<meta>This page transitions from the general meditative practice described previously to a specific ritual prayer of consecration. While the earlier text focused on internal illumination, this section provides the liturgical formula for blessing a sacred candle, invoking biblical figures to imbue the object with protective power.</meta>

<page-num>7.</page-num>
<warning>The ink is significantly faded throughout the page, making some characters difficult to distinguish.</warning>

O most holy Adonai <term>Adonai: A Hebrew title for God meaning "Lord," frequently used in medieval grimoires and liturgical prayers to emphasize divine sovereignty</term>, both powerful and wonderful, who led [the people] out of Egypt and who made all things with wisdom; you who multiplied your faithful servant Abraham and his seed like the stars of heaven; you who appeared to your servant Moses in the fire and flame of the bush, and revealed to him the ten [commandments] of the holy law—which he and Moses <note>The Latin text repeats "ipse et Moyses," which may be a scribal error or a specific rhetorical emphasis on the revelation</note> revealed; you who made the people of Israel walk across the sea with dry footsteps; you who gave the law to Moses on Mount Sinai; you who gave wisdom to the seed of Solomon, and especially you who mercifully revealed your divine salvation and the coming times to Christians: pour out your majesty upon us, and in this time, your sweet mercy, so that by your power this candle may be consecrated. Let it have, in this present moment and from now on, the power <term>virtutem: In a ritual context, this refers to the inherent spiritual or supernatural efficacy of a blessed object</term> which the most wise King Solomon bestowed upon it. Grant this, we pray you, most holy Adonai, Father and our King, remaining without end through the ages, and forever and ever. Amen.

And let this be said three times over that candle, and then with those spoken lights <note>original: "luminaria"; likely referring to the lit candles or the ritual lighting ceremony</note> on Friday and on Saturday; and let [the practitioner] speak when it is carried upon his person, and then neither by fire, nor by water, nor by war, nor by any injury will anyone be able to harm him.

<summary>This page contains a formal consecration prayer for a ritual candle, invoking God through the names and deeds of Abraham, Moses, and Solomon to grant the bearer protection from all physical harm.</summary>
<keywords>Adonai, Abraham, Moses, Solomon, consecration, ritual candle, protection charm, Solomonic tradition</keywords>

When you find any experiment <note>original: "experimentum"; in the context of medieval and Renaissance grimoires, this term refers to a specific magical procedure, recipe, or ritual act</note> where there will be a need for virgin paper <term>virgin paper: Latin "carta virginea." This refers to high-quality parchment or vellum made from the skin of a stillborn or fetal animal (usually a kid or lamb). It was considered "virgin" because the animal had never breathed or touched the earth, making the material spiritually "pure" for receiving sacred or magical ink</term>, you must understand [this to refer] to that membrane of a kid <note>a young goat</note> not yet born nor associated with the earth, but rather, before [it is skin], that kid [is] from the womb extra[cted] <unclear>that</unclear> <unclear>kid</unclear> <unclear>from</unclear> <unclear>the womb</unclear> <unclear>extracted</unclear> <note>The Latin text "extra-" appears to be the beginning of "extractus," meaning "extracted." The text breaks off here, likely describing the surgical removal of the fetus to ensure it never touches the ground.</note>

*   **Diagram 7:** Within the circle are three elongated, leaf-like vertical shapes. <note>These almond-shaped figures, often called "mandorlas" or "vesica piscis," frequently represent the intersection of the spiritual and material worlds or, more simply, seeds of divine potential.</note>

### Book One

### Concerning the Days and hours, for the necessary <term>points</term> <note>original: punctis; here referring to the specific requirements, details, or astrological "turning points" necessary for a rite</note>
### In all Magical experiments,
### and
### in what manner they are prepared

#### Chapter One

Every single one <note>original: una quaeque</note> of the hours of the day and night has a name, and each name belongs to its own master <note>original: dominis; referring to the planetary ruler of that specific hour</note> from among the seven planets, according to the same order of the planets described here.

<summary>This page introduces the first book of the manuscript, establishing the foundational rule that all magical operations must be timed according to the "planetary hours." It asserts that every hour of the day and night is governed by one of the seven classical planets.</summary>

### Concerning the planets

Between the seven <term>Stars</term> <note>original: Stellas; referring to the "wandering stars" or planets known to the ancient world</note> that wander, or <term>planets</term>, and the <term>Zodiac</term>, there is the <term>Firmament</term> <note>In medieval cosmology, the Firmament was the eighth sphere containing the fixed stars, situated above the planetary spheres.</note> in which the fixed <term>Stars</term> are located. But <term>Ptolemy</term> <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the influential Greco-Egyptian astronomer.</note> and <term>Alphonso</term> <note>Likely referring to King Alfonso X of Castile, who sponsored the "Alfonsine Tables" in the 13th century.</note> placed a <term>Crystalline Heaven</term> beneath the <term>Firmament</term>, above which is the <term>Empyrean Heaven</term> <note>The highest, most spiritual part of the universe, often described as a place of pure fire or light where the Divine resides.</note>. Indeed, the wandering <term>Stars</term> follow this order of the planets:

Wherefore <term>Saturn</term> is the first <term>Planet</term> in the order of the planets. <term>Saturn</term> rules the first <term>hour</term> of the day of the <term>Sabbath</term> <note>original: Sabbathi; Saturday</note> and of that entire first day. And <term>Jupiter</term> reigns over the second <term>hour</term> of the same day; <term>Mars</term> over the third <term>hour</term>; the <term>Sun</term> over the fourth <term>hour</term>; <term>Venus</term> over the fifth <term>hour</term>; <term>Mercury</term> over the sixth <term>hour</term>; the <term>Moon</term> over the seventh <term>hour</term>; and <term>Saturn</term> over the 8th <term>hour</term>. Of these rulers, <term>Jupiter</term> rules the 9th <term>hour</term>; <term>Mars</term> the 10th <term>hour</term>; the <term>Sun</term> the 11th <term>hour</term>; <term>Venus</term> the 12th <term>hour</term>; <term>Mercury</term> the 13th <term>hour</term>; the <term>Moon</term> the 14th <term>hour</term>; <term>Saturn</term> the 15th <term>hour</term>; <term>Jupiter</term> the 16th <term>hour</term>; <term>Mars</term> the 17th <term>hour</term>; the <term>Sun</term> the 18th <term>hour</term>; <term>Venus</term> the 19th <term>hour</term>; <term>Mercury</term> the 20th <term>hour</term>; the <term>Moon</term> the 21st <term>hour</term>; <term>Saturn</term> the 22nd <term>hour</term>; <term>Jupiter</term> the 23rd <term>hour</term>; and <term>Mars</term> the 24th <term>hour</term>.

The first [hour] of the following day belongs to the <term>Sun</term> <note>Sunday</note>, and so on for each of the individual hours and planets, for the <term>hours</term> of the day and night up to the 24th<gloss>original: 24am</gloss> hour.

Similarly, <term>Jupiter</term> then reigns, ruling the first hour of its own day <note>Thursday</note>; then <term>Mars</term> rules the first <term>hour</term> of its own day, the day of <term>Mars</term> <note>Tuesday</note>; then the <term>Sun</term> rules the first <term>hour</term> of its own day <note>Sunday</note>.

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page marks a significant shift in the manuscript from the physical construction of technical instruments (plates and materials discussed on the previous page) to the astrological timing required for their use. It introduces the system of "planetary hours," which dictates the success of specific magical or technical operations.</meta>

On Sunday <note>original: "Dominica"</note>, the second hour is of Venus, the third of Mercury, the fourth of Jupiter, the fifth of the Moon, the sixth of Saturn, the seventh of the Sun, the eighth again of the Moon, the ninth of Mars, the tenth of Mercury, the eleventh of Jupiter, and the twelfth of Venus. These are the hours of the day.

They are also found—by observing the same order and completing the circle in the order of the planets—if you begin from the second until the twenty-fourth, which is the last hour of the night. Then you will have at hand all the <term>experiments</term> <note>In this context, "experiments" (experimenta) refers to specific magical procedures, rituals, or technical trials.</note> or instructions which you ought to perform according to the course of the stars, and you shall accomplish them in your operations. 

Indeed, the hours of Saturn, Mars, and Mercury are the best for speaking to spirits, for magical and <term>necromantic</term> <note>The art of communicating with the dead or conjuring spirits.</note> acts, for sowing discords, and for spreading hatred; however, the aforementioned hours of Saturn and Mars ought to occur on the days over which that aforesaid form <note>Likely referring to the planetary "sign" or "influence."</note> rules. 

And [concerning] the first hour of any day—on whatever day you shall work in any hour, whether by day or by night—you shall observe in the aforesaid writings the certain hours through which you desire to have an "entrance" <note>An "introitum" or entrance likely refers to the starting point of a ritual or the moment a practitioner gains the attention of spiritual forces.</note>; then you should see at what time that specific hour arrives, and you shall perform it with such talent and grace. For you ought not to make a beginning <note>literally "head" (caput)</note> of all these days and aforesaid hours until the cycle has its completion and its end.

Furthermore, the hours of the Sun, Jupiter, and Venus—and on their specific days, whatever day you may be working—are the best for obtaining or carrying out all **experiments of Love**.

# The Little Key of Solomon concerning Spirits

### and concerning their operation, from which that key of working is seen, and which is said to be the foretold Wisdom of Solomon, and concerning sacred and divine names.

In this chapter it is declared how, in these **pentacles** <note>original: *pentaculis*; circular talismans used in ritual magic to command spirits or provide protection</note>, all the consecrated powers and the **Ineffable** <note>original: *Ineffabilia*; names of God considered too sacred to be spoken aloud</note> and holy and divine names of the whole of knowledge are hidden by God and for you, the complete knowledge of all things.

Those pentacles ought to be most pure, clean, and glorious, and written with these **Golden tablets** <note>original: *tabulis Aureis*</note>, and written through all Divine Revelation and <unclear>placed</unclear> in <unclear>gold</unclear>, and consecrated and sanctified through the powers of man and for the defense of body and soul. 

For this pentacle, having been made on the **Day of Mercury** <note>original: *Die Mercurij*; Wednesday</note> and in its [planetary] hour, with the **Moon** <note>original: *Luna*</note> being in an **Airy or Earthy sign** <note>original: *signo Aereo vel terreo*; refers to the zodiac signs categorized by elements, such as Gemini, Libra, Aquarius (Air) or Taurus, Virgo, Capricorn (Earth)</note> and on such a day; because if it is done on a day when the Moon is in the same sign, and on that day and with the other signs, let the Master perform it, or let him do the necessary things with pleasant and glowing things in <unclear>gold</unclear>. And on the **Day of Jupiter** <note>original: *die Jovis*; Thursday</note> in the hour of Mercury and the Moon, you must fumigate it with such fumigation as is described, in the presence of the Love of men and <unclear>everywhere</unclear> with water, as it is said, made from the water of **hyssop** <note>a plant used historically for ritual purification</note> and of <unclear>saffron</unclear> <note>original: *croco*</note>.

In the selection of a day, **Wednesday** <term>original: Dies Mercurij</term> is the day, or the 2nd hour which is the <term>second day</term>, and also in the first and third hours of the night which look toward the next **Matins** <note>The early morning liturgical hour, traditionally performed in the darkness before dawn</note>; and in those hours you will be able to complete all your **experiments** <note>Magical operations, rituals, or procedures</note> whether by day or by night, provided they have been arranged at the appropriate hours.

### In what manner the
### exorcist should conduct himself

### Chapter Two

The operator <note>original: actor</note> making a selection or performing experiments must think about which selection or experiment he desires to perform, and he must write that entire Art <note>the specific ritual instructions</note> on **virgin paper** <term>original: carta virginea; paper or parchment that has never been used for another purpose</term>, which is called **parchment** <note>original: carta Membrana; specifically prepared animal skin used for writing</note>. Afterwards, he must note on which day he ought to begin that Art or experiment; finally, he must consider in which place he wishes to perform the experiment. In this way, the **exorcist** <note>original: exorcizator; here used as a general term for the ritual practitioner</note> must take note of each and every thing that concerns such matters.

## Concerning Fasts and Observance
### chapter four

When the <term>necromancer</term><note>original: "Necromanticus". In this period, the term referred to a ritual magician who works with spirits, rather than the modern sense of a "death mage."</note> wishes to perform his work, and after all preparations have been made, he must first of all foster <term>observance</term><note>original: "custodia". He must abstain from all illicit things and every defilement of the body, from excess in food and drink, from all shameful speech and base games, from lust, and from the telling of idle tales. He should maintain as much decency within himself as possible, whether in his speaking, his walking, his drinking, or in all his actions. This must be observed for nearly nine days before the day of the Beginning, and his <term>Disciples</term><note>original: "Discipuli". Note that twice or thrice a day, for nine days before the Beginning of the work, the <term>Master</term><note>original: "Magister". > Lord God Almighty, look upon us your servants who are not worthy to speak your holy name; fill us with the grace of your spirit and enlighten us, for you called the <unclear>Canaanite woman</unclear><note>original: "Cananeam". Likewise, if anyone has sinned against Baptism—specifically if through depraved behavior they have provoked God, even through pride both in their dress and in their character—among fornicators, among those committing adultery both secretly and openly, in perjuries and in lies, in thefts, in robberies, in usury <note>original: "usuris," the practice of lending money at interest, which was considered a sin in the medieval church</note>, in hatreds both small and great, in slanders, in envies, in drunkenness, in gluttony, in [unseemly] actors and jesters, in those who worship idols, in those who consult diviners, in [reliance on] dreams, in sorcery <note>original: "Sortilegiis," specifically divination by the drawing of lots</note>, in witches <note>original: "Strijis," a term for malevolent female spirits or witches believed to harm people</note> and in every kind of divination; <meta>This page appears much later in the manuscript than the previous excerpt (page 77). # 795

Therefore, to "say farewell to sins" <note>original: "a peccatis vale dicere"</note> in the common tongue is, first, to renounce <unclear>wicked</unclear> habits on a set day, and even more so afterward, to diligently avoid all occasions of sin—whether of pleasure, or <unclear>living together</unclear>, or those involving the eyes or ears, and finally all of the senses.

This involves turning away from **Pride**, from **Greed**, from that which is as monstrous as it is beastly <note>The author uses "ferina" (beastly), likely referring to animalistic lusts or unrestrained rage</note>, from **Envy**, from **Gluttony**, from **Wrath**, from **Sloth** <term>Ignavia: a lack of spiritual or mental energy; a synonym for the sin of sloth</term> of the soul, and then from idleness, from bad company, from [bad] advice and persuasion, from evil speeches or words, and from places and persons that in any way lead toward sinning and <unclear>offending</unclear>. This applies both to the one who gives himself over to these things and the one who makes others liable to them.

On the contrary, [one must live] by **Humility**, by **Frugality** <term>parsimonia: thrift or moderation in spending and consumption</term>, by **Chastity**, by **Abstinence**, by **Patience**, by **Diligence**, and by **Prudence**. Finally, this is the quality and the precept against those things which are described in the common tongue, namely: [turning away] from [evil] thoughts, from [illicit] pleasure and illusion, from **Ignorance**, from **Weakness**, from all **Malice**, from the giving and receiving of illegal things, from [moral] laxity, from the harboring of evil, and from the deception of others.

Instead, [one follows] the path of obedience—or rather, the turning away from wicked adherence to sins. The precept is: do not detract from the good reputation of your neighbors; do not spread errors regarding their fame; do not diminish their glory or the truth. Truly, do not reveal a secret; do not deceive a friend. Keep the faith of the saints and of your lords; keep your word. Do not take away another's honor, but live with a chaste and pious heart, sharing goods and gifts. And just as one is able to have life by living, do not presume to do harm.

...by gently cherishing and mercifully helping <term>misericorditer: to act with a heart for the miserable; a core Christian virtue of compassion</term>, by obeying those nearby with reverence, by corresponding with the poor <note>original: "pauperibus"</note> and with guests, by avoiding worldly glory, and by sharing with neighbors as well as kinsmen and friends. One must not withdraw from owed services, [but rather live] by moving through society while [shunning] the proud, speaking gently with moderate and modest speech even to the lowly <note>original: "despicibus" – those who are looked down upon or of low station</note>. 

[This life is lived] by thinking from the intellect, by making known the mystery and the good <unclear>song</unclear> <note>original: "carola" – likely referring to a hymn of joy or a circle dance, here used metaphorically for a joyful heart</note> openly, by touching [the world] in silence and humility <term>humilitate: the virtue of groundedness and modesty</term>, and by performing the Divine Office through prayers and fasting <term>ieiunio: the spiritual practice of abstaining from food</term> day and night. 

[One lives] by the righteous judgments of God and by judging even through her [the soul], by rejoicing with exultations and joys in unsullied humility, and by [holy] sighs and tears <note>original: "fletibus" – a reference to the "gift of tears" in medieval spirituality, signifying deep repentance or devotion</note>. [One must live] with a studious trembling, [avoiding] the <unclear>softness</unclear> that glides in from vices, from sins, and from carnal concupiscence <term>concupiscentia carnali: an intense longing for physical or worldly pleasures that distract from the spiritual</term>, and from the delight of this world.

[Turn away] from the vices of hatred and hypocrisy, from envy, from insults, from lying, from causing strife, and from the illicit murmuring <note>original: "murmuratione" – a technical term in monastic life for grumbling or complaining behind others' backs</note> that glides in and disparages. [Turn away] from deceit, fear, and falsehood, and from the malice of sowing discord, from mockery, from cruelty, from malignity, from seduction, and from the poisonous detraction of [evil] habits. [Turn away] from blasphemy <term>blasphemia: irreverent or impious speech against God or sacred things</term>, from much-talking <note>original: "multiloquio" – excessive or idle chatter, often warned against in wisdom literature</note>, and from pretense.

[On the contrary, live] by vigils and devotion, and by perseverance through the seven sacraments <term>sacramenta: the seven sacred rites of the Church</term> of God, and through those seven [gifts of the Spirit], by the love of God <term>dilectione dei: the specific, selfless love directed toward the Divine</term> and the love of neighbor. [Live] by the will to obey them, living justly, joyfully, and peacefully, and by being strengthened in every venerable prayer and petition, in will and in action, in whatever belongs to <unclear>holiness</unclear>, and for those who possess it.

### Concerning the places where by Right these should be performed

"And let that which I commanded come forth."

### In what manner circles are made
### and how one should work within them

### Chapter Nine

How circles are made—namely three or more—in the place where the circle is to be made, with all its necessities, equipment <term>paramentis; ritual tools or ceremonial garments</term>, knives, and experiments <note>original: *experimentis*; in medieval grimoires, this term refers to specific magical procedures or spells rather than scientific trials</note>.

Take the aforementioned knife in hand and dig into the earth in the middle of the place where you wish to make the circle. Afterward, take a cord, and from the center of that circle, extend it into four parts toward the four quarters of the world—that is, the regions of the world: the south, the east, the west, the south <note>The original text repeats "meridiem" (south), which may be a scribe's error for the general four-fold orientation</note>, and the north. No other person [should be present] during this work. In the middle, place two swords <term>enses; ritual blades used to command spirits or define sacred space</term>, specifically the one you wish to use with the stated knife, or from the upper and northern part, marking <unclear>prohibiting</unclear> and indicating a cross to the right with the knife. You shall make another circle in its entirety with the said knife, and always dividing by individual sections, you shall make a circle between them at a distance of half a foot.

## On Water and Hyssop <term>hyssop: a plant used since biblical times for ritual purification and sprinkling holy water</term>
## and
## in what manner it should be worked

### How one works with wax or Virgin Earth

### Chapter Eighteen

In this book, nothing else in the experiments is worked in wax or earth except by making an image <note>image: often a symbolic figurine, poppet, or talisman used in sympathetic magic</note>, or a candle, or certain sacred vessels. It should be known that if someone performs these works in earth or wax, and it is not that which is "Virgin," [the work will fail]. If the earth and wax are Virgin, the truth <note>truth: here meaning the "true effect" or efficacy of the spell</note> is in this without corruption. Since earth and wax differ in condition, the aforementioned works concerning images would not reach their effect [without the Virgin quality]. Therefore, it is called "Virgin" when it is said that it has never been put to human use. "Virgin Earth" <term>Virgin Earth: "terra Virginea", soil that has never been dug, plowed, or used for burials or agriculture</term> is that very thing which is called "Adam’s earth" <note>Adam's earth: a reference to the primordial, uncorrupted soil from which God was said to have formed the first human</note> from which a person could be formed. Similarly, when anything is to be worked from this earth or from Virgin wax, let nothing else be conceived <note>conceived: intended or brought into being</note> unless there be spoken over it through prayer, exorcism, invocation, visitation, and His sacred name, so that it does not cause offense in itself; all things made of earth or...

# Concerning the Sacrifice

### Chapter 22

That Most Holy Eucharist is called the "host" <note>original: *hostia*, meaning a sacrificial victim.</note> from the act of slaughtering, although not in its own form but under another form <note>This refers to the theological concept of Christ being present under the forms of bread and wine rather than his human "species."</note>. It is also called a sacrifice because it is defined as a thing consecrated for divine use. Some, however, do not think so; but others say that although the slaughtering is not real, it must be sacramental, because that sacrifice is spoken of in relation to the Passion of Christ. Because in the Mass, the sacrifice must be sacrificed along with the passion of Christ, it is said to truly happen. He who says these things about the sacrifice in the most holy host [provides the basis] from which we must interpret this sacrifice. Therefore, the more exalted a thing is, the worse it becomes once it is corrupted <note>A reference to the Latin maxim *corruptio optimi pessima*: "the corruption of the best is the worst."</note> in the mouths of those [who speak of it]. The sacrifice in the elements [acts as] a mark <term>character<note>In theology, a "character" is an indelible spiritual mark; here it seems to relate to the mystical significance of the sacramental elements.</note></term> or the cause of the scriptures. Their end appears gilded and most clear, depicted before any sacrifice might come. Divine holiness holds that sacrifice in the host; through the sacrifice, it is given to God, and this is for all who are prepared for the same Mass. Afterward, that sacrifice of wonder is taken. Regarding this manner of the water-sacrificed <note>original: *aqua-sacrificato*; this may refer to the liturgical mixing of water and wine or perhaps a specific ritual cleansing.</note> and the reason is such because it ought to be done, etc.

And under this sixth [part] <note>original: *pane*; literally "bread," but likely a metaphorical reference to spiritual nourishment or a transcription error for *parte* (part)</note>, as it is called—specifically the first chapter from God regarding **wisdom** <note>original: *sapientia*</note>—all such things are gathered and concluded in brief; for they are briefly contained within the first section, namely concerning the **seven purities** <note>original: *septem munditiis*; ritual cleansings or moral purifications required of the practitioner</note> collected in seven chapters, specifically regarding the waiting of days and nights, etc. I say therefore, that for anyone who wishes to study from the Most High God, he will be taught so clearly in everything—even those things which are necessary—that it must be known in every operation he undertakes. In truth, under the aforementioned first chapter from God regarding wisdom, and under this sixth chapter, all the science of **philosophy** <note>original: *philosophie*; in this context, the term encompasses both natural science and the "occult" secrets of the universe</note> is briefly comprised; it is the final and supreme conclusion of the rule and of the whole **Art** <note>original: *Artis*; likely referring to a specific system of sacred magic or the *Ars Notoria*, a method for gaining knowledge through divine grace</note>. For this reason, this most holy book must be held with devotion.

### The end of the conclusion of the rule of the Art

### Here the entire light of the Art

### is happily acquired

->In peace<-

->Amen.<-

<summary>This page marks the conclusion of a significant section of the manuscript, asserting that all philosophical and magical knowledge flows from divine wisdom and ritual purity. It exhorts the practitioner to treat the text as a holy object and promises that total mastery of the "Art" is achieved here.</summary>

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page marks a significant shift in the manuscript from the practical geometry and instrument-making of the previous pages to a philosophical and metaphysical discussion. It begins a new section regarding the nature of "spirits"—a term that in this period could refer to biological vapors, the breath, or the soul itself.</meta>

# Treatise on spirits
## and their nature
### divided into three chapters

## Chapter one
### On the nature of spirits.

Air is a corruptible <term>element<note>original: "elementum corruptibile". In the science of this period, "corruptible" does not mean "evil," but rather "subject to change and decay," a property of all things found on Earth as opposed to the unchanging heavens.</note></term> throughout its whole, externally mobile, indivisible, and capable of receiving quantity; it is a smoky moisture and exists beneath the composite whole. For this reason, it is said that what is called "spirit" <note>original: "spiritus"</note> is what the intellectual soul is said to be—called nothing else but spirit, or what is called "living air." It comes from God just like light; just as light is said to be called <unclear>is</unclear> spirit. These are the elements according to the <unclear>earth</unclear> itself, endowed by God with its changing qualities. Thus it is carried, as if among the elements, by the influence of the planets into the earth, having emerged from the heart; it is simply called a thing which is indeed called light, and is so called in tribulation. And even if it is not granted to every place, it is called spirit equally for those <unclear>disposed</unclear> towards it; wherefore, whatever of their own <unclear>disposition</unclear> they are given for their work, the earth holds the spirit; equally, the spirit is then said to be from God, who speaks forever.

<summary>This page begins a new treatise defining "spirit" as a divine substance related to air and light, influenced by planetary movements and the intellectual soul. It characterizes air as a "living" element that mediates between God and the physical world.</summary>
<keywords>spirit, nature of air, intellectual soul, planetary influence, divine light, elements, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Treatise on Spirits" from page 224. While the previous page defined the physical and divine nature of "spirit" as an element related to air and light, this page transitions into Chapter Two, which classifies these spiritual entities (referred to here as "clerics") according to astrological influences and planetary alignments.</meta>

These are called "inductive" in relation to the renewal of the sun. <note>original: "Innovationi solis". This likely refers to the solar cycle or the sun's entry into a new sign.</note> There are three good ones, and even the settings [of stars] from which, when they have been clear, they are treated as almost indifferent; they are placed as nearly good when they align well with the stars of the third <note>This likely refers to the "third house" in astrology or a specific celestial grouping.</note> because their mistress and their works are arranged accordingly.

### On the vision of the spectacles
### of the clerics
### Chapter two

To these <term>clerics<note>original: "Clericis". In this context, the author is not referring to earthly priests, but to a hierarchy of spiritual beings or "servants" of the celestial order.</note></term>, a division applies according to the number of the seven **Planets** by which they are determined. This is because some are good and others are different—namely, some are far from others, and they differ as they are **eastern** or **western**; their goodness is spoken of in terms of their operations. Those who work toward this end by a higher power are named among the good and the bad. The bad ones are indeed **Southern** and **Northern**, and they are called "bad" because their works are evil and unclean—things which are said to be perceived by the voice and are foul. And so, they may perform some certain amount of good, or they are forced to do so by a higher power. To this end, there are other "collectors" who are said to be present in this world, and whoever is joined to them shapes their own powers, whether they be good or bad.

<summary>This page introduces Chapter Two of the treatise, classifying spiritual entities ("clerics") according to the seven planets and their cardinal directions. It suggests that these spirits' moral nature—whether good or evil—is dictated by their celestial positions and a higher governing power.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, planetary spirits, clerics, seven planets, spiritual hierarchy, solar renewal, eastern and western spirits</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion on the ethical selection of successors within the Church to a broader discussion of secular leadership, specifically focusing on the reciprocal relationship between a ruler and their subjects.</meta>

## On Gratitude toward Subjects

### Chapter Three

Because a King and Prince <note>In this context, "Prince" refers generally to a sovereign ruler or head of state.</note> administers his rule well not only through his own efforts but also through his subjects, he must therefore conduct himself toward them with a sense of reciprocity. As Ctesias <note>A Greek physician and historian from the 5th century BCE who wrote "Persica," a history of Assyria and Persia.</note> reports, if the King of the Persians knew that he would gain both utility and praise through gratitude toward his subjects, he would practice this by providing for them, protecting them, and appointing to their proper stations <note>original: "in proprio loco ponendo," meaning assigning roles or offices based on merit and status</note> whoever he judged worthy of his favor, according to their character and honor. He would grant them dignity and affection to increase their renown, showing favor and singular goodwill, thereby preserving the immortal memory of these men; for the King is said to celebrate their persons with honors and clearly through formal proclamations <note>original: "instrumentis," which likely refers to official decrees or the ceremonial "tools" of state governance</note>, as well as with great and generous gifts. On the other hand, the gratitude shown by the subjects consists of their obedience in their required service, their vigilance, their constancy, and their effort to act faithfully in every deed.

->**Gratitude**, **King of the Persians**, **Ctesias**,<-
->**Prince**, **Subjects**, **Rule**<-

<summary>This page discusses the necessity of mutual gratitude between a ruler and their subjects, using the King of the Persians as a historical model for rewarding merit and loyalty to ensure stable governance.</summary>
<keywords>kingship, subjects, gratitude, Ctesias, Persian Empire, reciprocity, governance, merit</keywords>
